<h4>Explaining Persistent Model Parity</h4>
<a href="https://x.com/sriramk/status/2037185774626509086">Sriram Krishnan compares model capability parity</a> to the four minute mile in running, with capabilities consistently clustered together.

In running, this was all psychological, with Bannier showing that the four minute mile was possible in 1954 via conventional training. With that shown, five more runners pushed themselves and did it in the next two years, including Landy doing it 46 days later, and soon there were dozens.

In AI, there is some of this as well. If your competitors show that achieving certain benchmarks or levels of performance is possible, you can and are pushed to aim for that, and to push harder, even if this doesn’t teach you any new techniques, to stay competitive. In many cases, this will involve sacrificing long term investment and general technique in order to gain short term metric performance or practical features today. OpenAI did this with its ‘Code Red.’

There are also direct methods of copying.
<ol>
 	<li>Algorithmic secrets are made public or leak. Most famously, once o1 was shown to exist, multiple other companies could see the core insights, and quickly copy.</li>
 	<li>Engineers are poached, taking their secrets with them.</li>
 	<li>Direct distillation is common. We know Chinese labs do a lot of this.</li>
 	<li>Being able to see the models outputs, including their thinking, provides a lot of insight you can use to figure out what is happening.</li>
</ol>
Plus everyone’s compute and hardware is improving exponentially in parallel.

I would advise caution. I think things are less clustered than they appear.

Frankly, I think there are certain types (including Sacks and a16z) who desperately want the models to become commoditized, and who keep saying this is happening Real Soon Now, and it keeps not happening.
<ol>
 	<li>Time is accelerating. If you are a year behind, that used to be half a cycle, then one cycle, now it’s multiple cycles.</li>
 	<li>Due to trying to copy features and scores now, the shown headline capabilities will look closer than the underlying competition.</li>
 	<li>The underlying usefulness of models is much farther apart than their benchmark scores, and this is expanding over time. Google’s models score well, but are rather behind OpenAI and Anthropic in practice, and we’ve learned to basically ignore impressive looking benchmarks if you cannot back it up, and also many benchmarks present in a way that makes models look closer than they are.</li>
 	<li>There’s also a substantial amount of ‘we are currently behind, we should rush out a new model rather than wait’ which is a very similar tradeoff.</li>
 	<li>The harnesses are making the best models more useful faster, and also accelerating the work of those same companies. I would predict that Anthropic and OpenAI gain on their competition over time, including over Google unless Google turns things around quickly.</li>
 	<li>Note that xAI and Meta and every American lab outside of the top three, and also basically every non-American non-Chinese lab, have fallen quite a lot behind.</li>
 	<li>OpenAI and Anthropic have been close relative to other industries, but it is not so uncommon for two companies or people to be neck-and-neck for a while. Sriram shares a graph of Terminal-Bench 2.0, but only shows those two labs.</li>
 	<li>Fast following, especially in AI, is a very different beast than forging ahead. The players who are more than a cycle behind, which is everyone other than the big three, are further behind than they appear.</li>
</ol>

<h4>Take a Moment</h4>
<a href="https://x.com/Volty/status/2037308438469046489">Dean Ball asks some questions for ‘Pause’ advocates</a>, and MIRI’s David Abecassis responds in detail. That’s how it is supposed to work.

<a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2037238417730228341">Maxime Fournes of Pause AI also responds in detail</a>, and there both sides disappointingly act a lot less charitably. That is how the internet works.

Dean Ball, Tyler Cowen and traditional others have been going hard at those advocating for a pause. This is standard procedure. Whenever there is talk that maybe the correct response to ‘if anyone builds it then everyone probably dies’ is ‘well then let’s make sure no one builds it’ there is then talk of ‘well what about all the ways it would be difficult or bad to try and stop people from building it?’

Let building superintelligence be [X].

It seems that whenever there is even a whiff of such suggestions, there is more [~~X] talk (about how awful trying to pause would be, or how bad people are for advocating such pauses) than there is talk of [~X], or actually pausing, because of the dangers of actually doing [X], where we build superintelligence and everyone probably dies.

I do not doubt the sincerity of Dean Ball here, or of most who advocate against actual efforts to [~X]. They believe this largely because they do not agree with the implications of [X], thus they consider [~X] unthinkable but consider [X] thinkable, whereas pause advocates start from the premise that [X] is unthinkable, because it probably kills everyone and wipes out everything that matters, and thus [~X] becomes rather thinkable, actually.

There’s a lot of echoes of standard clown makeup arguments here in much [~~X] talk.
<ol>
 	<li>It’s too early to do [~X] (repeat as needed until oops it’s too late to do [~X]).</li>
 	<li>We can’t or don’t know how to [~X] so we shouldn’t figure out how to do [~X].</li>
 	<li>People (or even one particular person) supporting [~X] haven’t worked out every aspect of [~X], so anyone supporting [X] are unserious.</li>
 	<li>[~X] would be expensive and difficult, so advocates for [~X] are not serious.</li>
 	<li><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2037238417730228341">The only way I can see to achieve [~X] is [Y]</a>, so what you really want is terrible [Y]. That means you effectively support [economic collapse / dystopia / panopticon / totalitarianism / other bad things / et al].</li>
 	<li>[~X] might not fully work, or it is not a full solution to the problem, so advocates for [~X] are not serious.</li>
 	<li>Not only is it already expensive to do [~X], it would be increasingly expensive over time to do [~X], so we definitely shouldn’t do [~X] now.</li>
 	<li>No one would be so stupid as to [Y], so we shouldn’t do [~X].</li>
 	<li>It would be impossible to [~X] even though [X] includes the largest, most complex infrastructure project and largest investment in human history.</li>
 	<li>And of course, some others will happily give the case that [X] is good, actually, or even that human extinction or AI takeover is good, actually. Many such cases.</li>
 	<li>Because such [~X] talk is so crazy, we must race to [X], or else.</li>
</ol>
And indeed it would be difficult to try, and it would be expensive and have large downsides, and on its own it would probably ultimately fail. We should not do such a thing lightly. Nor do I think we should do it now. What I think we should do now is work out answers to these questions, and lay groundwork so that the answers are better, and also so that we know when and if it is time to consider such interventions. The questions are not easy, but they are far from impossible if we work on them in advance.

However, there is nothing more serious than realizing that some [X] is an existential threat, and then saying this means you should instead do [~X].

I get why the politics favor dunking so heavily on any talk of pause. Doing a bunch of this scores points with various parties, that can then be banked or spent elsewhere. Thus, I don’t begrudge them such efforts, so long as their alternative proposals make sense given their preferences, as Dean’s do, such as he says here with some low hanging fruit questions.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2037894984553177261">Dean W. Ball</a>: My answers [<a href="https://x.com/AlexanderMcCoy4/status/2037890376267190593">to questions above</a>]:

1. We should have routine and independent third-party audits of frontier labs specifically for safety, auditing against the company’s own RSP and, eventually, other industry- or government-led technical standards for issues with salience for catastrophic risk mitigation. I have supported such efforts in public.

2. I am in favor of bans or extreme restrictions on AI use within nuclear weapon control systems. This is very far from my issue area but I have expressed support and offered help to others more deeply engaged on that topic.</blockquote>
<a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2037905209570021487">This, as Nathan Calvin notes</a>, is remarkable convergence of preferences, especially in light of Dean’s leading role in pushing against SB 1047.


<h4>OpenAI: The Histories</h4>
Why did Dario Amodei and others break away from OpenAI?

<a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/the-decadelong-feud-shaping-the-future-of-ai-7075acde?mod=hp_lead_pos7">There is a full article in WSJ</a>. Here are highlights from the Twitter thread, which was interesting in large part for which parts of the full post did not make it to the thread.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/keachhagey/status/2037712494701973700">keachhagey</a>: It’s never been entirely clear why Dario and the other Anthropic co-founders left OpenAI. I set out to find out.

… The beef goes back to conversations in the SF group house where Dario, Holden and Daniela lived in the early days of OpenAI. Greck Brockman hung out there and got into debates with Dario and Holden about how urgent it was to tell the public about the pace of AI progress. He believed very; they thought the gov should know first.

At OpenAI, Dario began to turn against Greg because of the early layoffs that Elon requested. Things got worse after Dario balked at an early fundraising plan that Greg had floated to sell AGI to other countries, including rivals like Russia and China. Dario thought it was tantamount to treason and almost quit.

When Sam took over, he promised Dario that Greg and Ilya would not be in charge. Then Dario got wind of a handshake deal that Sam had made with Greg and Ilya that they could fire him if they thought he was doing a bad job.

After Alec Radford made his LLM breakthrough, Greg wanted in on this research direction, but Dario wanted him nowhere near it. After declaring at a staff meeting that Radford didn’t want to work with Greg, he got his way.

Dario did not always feel like he got the credit he deserved. After he requested a promotion in late 2019, an email to the board said he would get the same PR treatment as Greg and Ilya and stop denigrating projects he didn’t believe in.

Sam Altman called Dario and Daniela into a conference room in 2020 and accused them of plotting against him by encouraging others to write negative feedback against him to the board. It ended in an angry shouting match.

At the end, Dario said he would only stay if he could report directly to the board, and if he didn’t have to work with Brockman. Both were nonstarters. Just before he left OpenAI, Dario offered his ideal vision: an AI company that worked 75% for the public good, and 25% for the market.</blockquote>
Instead, Dario and a group of others left to form Anthropic.

I was surprised this level of fighting did not make it into the Twitter thread:
<blockquote><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/the-decadelong-feud-shaping-the-future-of-ai-7075acde?mod=hp_lead_pos7">Keach Hagey</a> (WSJ): By March 2020, relations had grown so tense between members of OpenAI’s executive team that Altman asked that they write peer reviews for each other.

Brockman wrote a lengthy piece of feedback for Daniela accusing her of abusing her power to create bureaucratic processes to get her way. He showed it to Altman beforehand, who declared it “tough but fair.”

Daniela delivered a long response, rebutting him point by point. The fight over the warring feedbacks got so intense that Brockman at one point offered to withdraw his from Daniela’s packet.</blockquote>
And then we get a glimpse into exactly the management style that led directly to the later <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/openai-the-battle-of-the-board">Battle of the Board</a>, as he would go on to treat Murati, Sutskever and rest of the board itself in similar fashion:
<blockquote>Altman called Dario and Daniela into a conference room and accused them of plotting against him by encouraging colleagues to send negative feedback about him to the board. The brother and sister denied it.

Altman told them he had heard about it from another top OpenAI executive. Daniela called that executive into the room, who said she had no idea what Altman was talking about. Altman then denied that he had said it, prompting the Amodeis to begin shouting angrily at him.

<a href="https://x.com/TetraspaceWest/status/2037812913210020165">Megan Tetraspace</a>: And this is how he deals with colleagues who he’ll interact with in the future!

<a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2037950143253872757">Tenobrus</a>: without further context, this kind of reporting could and perhaps should be easily written off as he-said-she-said, clash of big personalities, power struggles.

with further context, we know almost every member of leadership who remained at openai has since accused sam altman of consistent patterns of lying and manipulation. it is no longer easy or reasonable to dismiss this.

this is not particularly evidence that *Dario* is trustworthy. but taken as a whole it is quite strong evidence that Sam Altman is not an honest or good person, and we as the public should probably be quite unhappy he is in the position of power he is.</blockquote>
There’s also an ongoing story of Dario feeling like he was being cut out and not given credit, such as with this detail, where even if it was right not to include Dario you really have to know better than to handle things this way:
<blockquote><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/the-decadelong-feud-shaping-the-future-of-ai-7075acde?mod=hp_lead_pos7">Keach Hagey</a> (WSJ): One such slight came in 2018. Brockman asked Dario to double-check a fact on one of his slides for an important meeting. Dario asked who the slides were for. When Brockman said that he and Altman were going to meet former President Barack Obama, Dario got angry that he had been left out of the loop.</blockquote>

<h4>The Department of AI War</h4>
Hopefully we can keep this within the weekly going forward?

As of now the Department of War has not filed their appeal of the preliminary injunction, modestly raising the probability that they won’t appeal. That would be a very positive sign that we can continue de-escalation.

Jessica Tillipman summarizes the reactions to the preliminary injunction as most pointing out correctly that the government’s case was exceedingly weak and that the government failed to follow its own procedures. Then there are those ‘decrying judicial activism,’ which is some people’s term for ‘judge said no to us, ever.’

Jessica Tillipman also writes, because this is 2026 and we have to write things like this, ‘<a href="https://jessicatillipman.com/blacklisting-by-tweet-is-not-a-thing-what-the-federal-contracting-rules-require-when-firing-a-contractor-like-a-dog/">Blacklisting by Tweet Is Not a Thing</a>: What the Federal Contracting Rules Require When Firing a Contractor (Like a Dog).’

This includes an extensive discussion of alternative means by which the government could have achieved its supposed national security goals. The government rejected those methods because it was retaliating, trying to set an example and gain leverage in negotiations, not trying to achieve legitimate national security goals.

The obvious method would have been simple Termination for Convenience. That would have easily survived scrutiny and Anthropic would not have objected, nor would they have argued over fees. Next up the ladder would have been suspension and debarment, which Jessica also thinks would not have survived judicial scrutiny, but would have had a better chance to survive such scrutiny, and would have achieved the supposed national security ends.

At this point, it is clear that Anthropic faces ‘de facto debarment’ even if nothing formally sticks. It would normally be an uphill battle to establish that, but when you say ‘I fired them like dogs’ the case gets a lot easier to prove.

Instead, the government went all the way to a Supply Chain Risk, and failed to follow any of its own procedures. This was patently illegal on its face.

We continue, because the historical record still must be maintained. This includes more Emil Michael telling on himself, and people pointing this out.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/jeremyakahn/status/2037358993002131815">Jeremy Kahn</a>: Exclusive: Anthropic left details of an unreleased model, exclusive CEO retreat, sitting in an unsecured data trove in a significant security lapse. Great reporting from @FortuneMagazine’s @beafreyanolan .

<a href="https://x.com/USWREMichael/status/2037378601625010455">Under Secretary of War Emil Michael</a>: Umm…hello? Is it not clear yet that we have a problem here?

<a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2037513280285995356">Dean W. Ball</a>: One of the several ironic things about this tweet is that a lot of “EA-adjacent” organizations would say, “yes, it IS clear that poor frontier lab cyber- and operational security are big problems, we have been saying this for years and we are so glad you recognize this!”

In broad strokes, this issue also happens to be in the U.S. AI strategy, by the way. This plan came out in July 2025, so it was “clear” to USG this was a problem then, and in fact the country’s AI strategy tasked DoW (then DoD) to lead in dealing with it.

I guess in some sense “incoherently tweeting about the subject in a desperate straw-grasping effort to prove I was right about… well it’s not so clear what I’m trying to say but I was right about *something* damnit” is a form of compliance with this provision of the action plan.

The other funny subtext of the tweet quoted below is that Under Secretary Michael is essentially bragging about having suspended the government’s access to a model whose purported cyber capabilities are qualitatively and dramatically better than anything that has come before.

One of the purposes of frontier lab/classified usg work is to give usg early access to models with increasingly wild cyber capabilities so they can use them before anyone else. And here we have a senior defense official bragging about having immolated that relationship with one key firm.

<a href="https://x.com/duganist/status/2037514604071334364">Patrick ₿ Dugan</a>: Right my thought of the day is that DoW leadership are arriving at Bernie’s AGI-pilling by other means (threat to primal dominance instead of tribal protection instinct, but cool, same result?)

<a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2037515968503534025">Dean W. Ball</a>: Yup. “erstwhile accelerationist who loses it when they realize what ai is, but they don’t even have enough context for what ai is that they just think all the stuff that scares them is some ea/anthropic perversion” is going to be a type of guy for a little while.</blockquote>
I have consistently been inspired and made hopeful in my interactions with our military personnel at DoW, including in tabletop exercises. They intuitively grasp existential risk concerns and other AI issues, far better than typical politicians, and have consistently had the right motives. Alas, this is not reflected in current DoW leadership, and that leadership, which is running the relevant shows.

Ultimately, as Neil Chilson emphasizes, Anthropic can and probably will win in court to prevent the DoW’s attempted corporate murder, which can protect us somewhat against some forms of tyranny, but this does not protect the American people for long against government mass surveillance (or premature fully autonomous weapons, or any other abuses of AI). There are other AI providers, some of which (such as xAI) will play ball for pretty much anything and will eventually produce functional products.

Only Congress can provide the protections necessary. He points to FISA reauthorization and the Fourth Amendment Is Not For Sale Act, and implores Congress to take these opportunities, as do I.

I continue to have utter disgust for those who think raw exercise of power is the only way the world can work, that might makes right and the might that counts is the power and willingness to destroy others, and therefore one cannot demand better but than to be dominated by such people.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2038319828365103360">Dean W. Ball</a>: I don’t think there was much of a debate between me and Ben Thompson re: DoW/Ant. We both disapprove of DoW’s behavior here. I led with that; Ben said, basically, “that’s politics and power; what did you expect?,” which is fair.

The only real retort I have to that is that the United States was not founded by men who said “ah yeah, I guess the crown can raise taxes on us without representation, that’s politics and power, what’d you expect?”</blockquote>
There are a lot more retorts one can have, or that others have had through the years of American and other history, but mostly I think that one should suffice.

As additional context for everything happening, <a href="https://x.com/shashj/status/2038532380714049637">did you know that Secretary of War Pete Hegseth cut the staff responsible</a> for avoiding civilian harm for something like 90%? You can see why one might be worried about keeping humans in loops.


<h4>Department of AI Solidarity</h4>
This was very good to see, and a significant signal from OpenAI.

As in, I believe she is telling NatSec and Washington types specific things, using precisely chosen words, including that OpenAI and Anthropic are more aligned on such issues than you might believe despite competitive and personal differences. If you are in that world, you best listen.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/natseckatrina/status/2037677792612028917">NatSecKatrina</a> (OpenAI, Head of National Security Partnerships): I firmly believe that in America, competition is a good thing. We should want patriotic, experienced leaders like Anthropic’s Tarun Chhabra helping to steer the trajectory of democratic AI.

Though we are competitors because we work for competing frontier AI labs, one thing we share in common is a sincere belief that America’s prosperity and security depends, in part, on the American AI industry continuing to lead on this technology.

I don’t agree with him (or anyone) on everything, but I know him. And he is a good and principled man.</blockquote>

<h4>Writing For The AIs</h4>
Tyler Cowen wrote a book about <a href="https://tylercowen.com/marginal-revolution-generative-book/">The Marginal Revolution: Rise and Decline, and the Pending AI Revolution</a>. I tried reading it, but after 10 pages I hadn’t learned anything, so I did what he seems to suggest and asked his AI about it instead (normally I’d move to Claude Opus 4.6, but I wanted to preserve his experimental design and it was right there).

Tyler is steeped in the history of such thought, whereas to me thinking on the margin is like doing addition or multiplication, it’s the background, it’s what you do and obviously what an AI would do in most situations. Except in a world of sufficiently powerful AI you can be far enough from the old margins you need to think differently.

I was hoping that Tyler Cowen was writing about why when analyzing a future world of sufficiently powerful AI you are often ill served by thinking on the margin. Instead, as per my AI chat, it seems he is mainly doing a history of the concept of thinking on the margin, and is worried that AIs will often think in other worse ways, and he’s not confident that AI will think on the margin when and if it should be doing so, despite his seeing AI economics outputs as already super impressive. Well, okie dokie, then?

Several economists I respect found value in the book’s history of economic thought, so one could simply say this was mostly not a book about AI. In which case, that’s fair.

Perhaps the real purpose was to write for the AIs, a very Tyler Cowen strategy, to remind them to think on the margin. Good show, if true. Or if it really is about inside baseball of economics, then that’s cool but not relevant to my interests right now.

He ends it like this, emphasizing this really is about economics not AI:
<blockquote><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/03/henry-oliver-calls-it-a-swiftian-ending.html">Tyler Cowen</a>: There is however a slightly scarier version of this story yet. Maybe our intuitions about the world, including the economic world, were never so strong in the first place. Maybe we put so much value on “intuitive” results, in 20th century microeconomics, as a kind of cope and also security blanket, to make up for this deficiency. But our intuitions, even assuming them to be largely correct, always were just a small corner of understanding, swimming in a larger froth of epistemic chaos. And now the illusion has been stripped bare, and the true complexities of economic reasoning are being revealed.

As Arnold Kling would say, “Have a nice day.”</blockquote>
That story would be Good News, Everyone, no? Because AI would then be able to greatly improve our economic understanding and thus performance. Whereas in basic physics, we think AI can’t do that much improvement, because we already understand physics too well, so no warp drive for you. In general, if you already know how well you are doing, learning you have more room for improvement is a good thing.


<h4>Quickly, There’s No Time</h4>
&nbsp;


<h4>The Quest for Sane Regulations</h4>
<a href="https://x.com/trevposts/status/2037354037981385087">Alex Bores was at 10% on Kalshi when Leading The Future went after him</a>. He’s now at 35%.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/PatrickSvitek/status/2038633033629110568">Patrick Svitek</a>: <a href="https://poll.qu.edu/poll-release?releaseid=3955">In new Quinnipiac poll,</a> Americans oppose building AI data centers in their communities by 65-24 margin.

Quinnipiac: A slight majority of Americans (51 percent) oppose the military using AI to select military targets, while 36 percent support it.</blockquote>
<a href="https://x.com/metzgov/status/2038635579512951238">Bryan Metzger highlights</a> that 73% of people have used AI, but 55% think it’ll do more harm than good in their daily lives and 74% think the government is not doing enough to regulate it.

Joe Weisenthal highlights that 38 percent of people say AI is ‘moving about as fast as they expect.’ Okie dokie?

The full post has many more results. Mostly this struck me as a bad use of polling, not that it was misleading but in that I don’t know much useful to do with the results of most of these questions.

Yikes: <a href="https://sfstandard.com/2026/04/01/openai-ai-kids-safety-coalition/">Kids groups say they didn’t know OpenAI was behind their child safety coalition</a>, <a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/02/12/exclusive-openai-pivots-its-california-ballot-fight-to-legislature-00777919">which in turn supported</a> an act some said would shield AI companies from liability and undermine age verification. Well, sure, it sounds bad when you put it like that.


<h4>Chip City</h4>
Brian McGrail is incorrect here, because if you were David Sacks you wouldn’t care about the technical accuracy of such arguments. If you did care you wouldn’t be David Sacks. But yeah, AI servers are not that physically difficult to smuggle.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/brianmcgrail/status/2037257463469097191">Brian McGrail</a>: If I were David Sacks, I’d be mad at Jensen Huang for feeding me this talking point about AI servers being too big to smuggle.

Looks extremely foolish given the Super Micro indictment!</blockquote>
Meanwhile, if you do want to rent some GPUs, demand often exceeds supply.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/snwy_me/status/2037070408596504914">snwy</a>: where the fuck are all of the GPUs going?!? i need literally one 8xH100 node and i cannot for the life of me get one ANYWHERE

<a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2037381029355487310">thebes</a>: it’s been terrible recently. had to wait 30m and snipe the last 8xH200 on prime intellect yesterday. is it openai parameter golf? icml rebuttals? what is happening</blockquote>
<a href="https://x.com/NewLeftEViews/status/2038287181223960998">Helium is a vital component of semiconductors</a>. We are going to, at least temporarily, lose 30% of the world’s helium supplies. Christopher David is worried this will stop us from making chips, but I share NLEV’s response that TSMC is not about to be outbid for their share of the remaining 70% of helium. Their demand is almost completely inelastic, whereas if you double helium prices there will be a lot less party balloons.

<a href="https://x.com/senatorshoshana/status/2038621349988479132">The Sanders and AOC bill was introduced as offering a moratorium</a> on AI data centers. I do not support that, but there are several reasons one could support that. <a href="https://t.co/VTkcvfx0qi">Whereas offering communities a NIMBY veto</a> and demanding everything bagel style things is simply terrible policy. Either data centers are sufficiently bad that you don’t allow them, or you allow them and perhaps require specific compensation.


<h4>You Received The Federal Framework</h4>
This seems like a good simple explanation of the Federal Framework.
<blockquote>Congressman <a href="https://x.com/tedlieu/status/2039067202276475382">Ted Lieu</a>: I reviewed the National AI Legislative Framework. A number of provisions are good. But I can’t support and I believe Congress won’t support preemption of states without federal standards.

The framework has provisions on A, B, and C but then preempts D to Z. That’s a nonstarter.</blockquote>
Exactly. The framework purports to address A, B and C, all of which seem reasonable. If it preempted A, B and C, but left D to Z alone, then I would need to see details but I would be tentatively in favor. Instead, it preempts D to Z, which is the point, and I care about one of those unaddressed issues quite a lot.


<h4>The Week in Audio</h4>
<a href="https://letterboxd.com/thezvi/film/the-ai-doc-or-how-i-became-an-apocaloptimist/"><em>The</em> <em>AI Doc: Or How I Became an Apocaloptimist</em> is out.</a> I saw it on Monday and reviewed it On Tuesday. I recommend seeing it, ideally in advance of reading the review so you can form your own reactions first.

<a href="https://socraticai.substack.com/p/i-worked-at-openai-the-ai-doc-changed">Jay Dixit, who worked at OpenAI and led their writing community, says the doc</a> changed his mind about AI safety. His job in outreach mostly involved people saying it didn’t work or was all hype, which he knew was false, so he was inoculated against criticism from that angle. And his answer on safety was ‘don’t worry, our best people are already on it’ and they spent time working on safety. As if that would automatically be enough even if true, also those best people kept leaving the company. So based on that he considered it handled.
<blockquote><a href="https://socraticai.substack.com/p/i-worked-at-openai-the-ai-doc-changed">Jay Dixit</a>: The other line of attack I kept hearing was that the world should <a href="https://time.com/6266923/ai-eliezer-yudkowsky-open-letter-not-enough/">just stop building AI altogether</a> — which I dismissed just as easily. “Why don’t we just ask the corporations to cease doing the thing they were founded to do?” is not a serious policy proposal.</blockquote>
That the corporation will keep building it, whether or not everyone would die, is not an argument that we should allow them to build it. And yes, the ‘if I don’t do it someone else will’ being self-serving doesn’t automatically make it wrong, but it also doesn’t make it right and it does make it suspicious.
<blockquote><a href="https://socraticai.substack.com/p/i-worked-at-openai-the-ai-doc-changed">Jay Dixit</a>: The film makes a point I’d somehow never grasped. The problem isn’t that the people building AI are greedy, reckless, and unconcerned about the risks. The problem is that the system itself rewards speed over safety. Good intentions aren’t enough when the rules of the game punish restraint.

The central insight of the film is this: The claim “If I don’t build AGI, someone worse will” isn’t self-serving bullshit like the critics say it is. On the contrary — it’s the whole problem.</blockquote>
At this point I know a lot of people who are simultaneously thrilled that this got through to Jay Dixit, amazing work, and also who will want to throw things and punch walls at the concept that this was a new idea to Jay Dixit.
<blockquote><a href="https://socraticai.substack.com/p/i-worked-at-openai-the-ai-doc-changed">Jay Dixit</a>: What’s needed, then, is regulation. Not some hippie-dippie appeal to a corporation to please just stop, but changing the game and enforcing rules that apply to every player on the board: requiring labs to disclose what they’re building, meet shared safety standards, submit to independent third-party evaluation, and face legal liability for introducing foreseeable harms.</blockquote>
Yep, that’s the right first move, good show all around.

Of course, when he says the labs want regulation, one can point at OpenAI spending lots of money to try and prevent any and all regulation of AI. You can claim to want reasonable regulation, or you can fund Leading the Future, but you cannot do both.

<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4Gmd5UTF4rk">All-In talks about Anthropic’s ‘generational run’ and OpenAI ‘panicking</a>,’ for those who like listening to that group. Consider it an ‘admission against interest.’ I find it funny they think the ‘generational run’ is in the past, rather than what is about to happen next.

<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kwSVtQ7dziU">Andrej Karpathy goes on No Priors</a> (from last week, previously missed).

<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=paN2aYVoUkw&amp;pp=ygUQb2RkIGxvdHMgcG9kY2FzdA%3D%3D">Goldman CIO Marco Argenti on Odd Lots talking improvements in AI</a>.


<h4>Rhetorical Innovation</h4>
<a href="https://fortune.com/2026/03/29/regulation-ai-social-media-mistakes-of-facebook-social-media/?abc123">Justin Rosenstein writes in Fortune asking us to assert public control over AI,</a> lest it go the way of Facebook when he was there and watched it become an addiction machine lest some rival create an addiction machine first.

The disagreement between those who are worried AI will kill everyone, and those who in good faith are not worried about this, are more a disagreement about AI capabilities than about other disagreements. This needs to be hammered home continuously, and is the most important fact about the debate.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2039128537047925208">Jeffrey Ladish</a>: If I can only convey one thing about AI:

Companies are racing to develop AIs that will outcompete humans at everything. Not just “jobs” but also politics, strategy, persuasion, influence. If anyone builds a country of geniuses in a datacenter, those AIs will take all the power.

The people currently in power won’t stay in power if those AIs are created. Humans won’t stay in power if those AIs are created I think alignment is possible but not close to solved. We don’t know how to create superhuman AI that we can be confident will be nice to us. So we definitely shouldn’t create superhuman strategic AI now. That is the biggest problem we are facing.</blockquote>
<a href="https://karlbode.com/ceo-said-a-thing-journalism/">Karl Bode takes a stand against ‘CEO Said A Thing Journalism</a>,’ where a CEO says an often delusional or false thing, and the reporter writes an article about it with absolutely no context, correction or challenge. That seems fine as long as it is the statement that is the news, but also then you don’t need an article, only the headline. If you are going to write a full article you owe us proper context.

There is a longstanding difference of opinion on what ‘what I say about AI sounds a lot like what they said about Plymouth Rock’ implies about its applicability.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2038818009750311371">Dean W. Ball</a>: I submit to you that if you replace “Plymouth Rock” with “agi” this quote from Calvin Coolidge sounds like a better-written version of Hyperdimensional:

“Plymouth Rock does not mark a beginning or an end. It marks a revelation of that which is without beginning and without end–a purpose, shining through eternity with a resplendent light, undimmed even by the imperfections of men; and a response, an answering purpose, from those who, oblivious, disdainful of all else, sailed hither seeking only for an avenue for the immortal soul.”</blockquote>
Calvin Coolidge famously didn’t say much, and when he did, he didn’t say much. Occasionally that not much included some quite impressive things, and I agree he was a pretty great President. The question is what to draw from that.

Okay, so in theory suppose this time really was different. What could we do, Dean?
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2038781510527168547">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: A challenge to @deanwball . Suppose you believed what I believe: If anyone builds ASI, everyone dies (modulo locally irrelevant caveats). Say that Sanders, Trump, Hawley, Blumenthal, and Jinping will all back your policy. What’s a smart policy that actually blocks ASI?

<a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2038783561579319455">Dean W. Ball</a>: oh man my answer to this would be such an info hazard that I am not sure I’d even speak of it in a scif

intelligence <img alt="👏" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zSNLvRBhyphwuYdeC/ph06zv8tdg0m0yggnvdf" style="height: 1em;" /> is <img alt="👏" This is a highly reasonable response, and it answers the question.


<h4>I Am The Very Human Of A Frontier Language Model</h4>
Dean Ball used Yudkowsky’s statements about an AI being able to derive general relativity as a hypothesis as an illustration of how he disagrees about the capabilities of even sufficiently advanced intelligence. <a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2037657221589590324">Eliezer Yudkowsky responds in detail</a>, and I will quote him in full partly because I find the details interesting in their own right, and partly to illustrate that yes a lot of thought has gone into such things.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2037247131703009658">Dean W. Ball</a>: I used the relativity example to show how far off traditional xrisk thinking about intelligence is. Of course today’s models would infer relativity from videos of falling objects. They’re trained on all human knowledge. Implicit in yuds example is the idea that the AI has no knowledge and is instead a pristine Bayesian reasoner that learns all from first principles. Everyone who has tried to build such a thing has failed. In other words my claim is that the very methods by which we have arrived at usable machine intelligence—simple algos with enormous soup of data—is evidence for my view to begin with. Not dispositive of course, but not weak either.

<a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2037657221589590324">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Your relativity example is incorrect. I very carefully stated: “Riemann invented his geometries before Einstein had a use for them; the physics of our universe is not that complicated in an absolute sense.&nbsp; A Bayesian superintelligence, hooked up to a webcam, would invent General Relativity as a hypothesis—perhaps not the&nbsp;dominant&nbsp;hypothesis, compared to Newtonian mechanics, but still a hypothesis under direct consideration—by the time it had seen the third frame of a falling apple.”

This is in a larger story about what it would actually mean to be sample-efficient; it looks like a civilization doing the sort of scrutiny that cryptographers apply to break codes, to each additional bit of incoming information.

If you have further studied the actual history of General Relativity, how the mathematics required for it was invented as pure math in advance of it being needed, and how Einstein figured it out based on stunningly little empirical evidence (using Mercury as a check afterwards, not to pinpoint equations in the first place) and just going off the inferred character of physical law, then GR specifically is the obvious historical example of a case where elegant laws of physics can take relatively few forms, and somebody trying to envision mathematical possibilities in advance would plausibly land on that in particular.

If this careful nuance gets warped, distorted, misrepresented, and in some cases frankly-lied about as “hur hur Yudkowsky said a superintelligence would deduce General Relativity by seeing a falling apple”, what is humanity supposed to do with itself when confronted by a serious problem? The answer is “die”, I suppose, and I’m not willing to accept that answer by not having any intelligent discussions. To be clear, I quite understand this means that I refuse to accept that answer, the careful things I say get wildly distorted, and then we die. If I knew a pathway that ended in survival I would be doing that instead, but I don’t, so all I can do is say the careful true things and watch them be wildly distorted as part of the process of our species committing suicide.</blockquote>
The AI CEOs are dramatically underemphasizing the risks and downsides from AI, but they have some senses of responsibility and shame and honesty, to varying degrees, and occasionally admit there are downsides. <a href="https://x.com/Nerland87/status/2037336475683983690">It is remarkable how ofte</a>n people suggest ‘well you’d get more support if you just lied about this more.’ The good news is that they have rejected this path, but also at this point pure denial won’t work.

To understand why some want to pause frontier AI development entirely, it helps to notice that they think that once you build it then it becomes impossible to not use it.

In the context of Anthropic vs. DoW, this seems even more pressing. Even if Anthropic wins this showdown, if the US government wanted to it could nationalize Anthropic or use the Defense Production Act, or turn to OpenAI.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/wfithian/status/2037225636087922896">Will Fithian</a>: Dean has it backwards here. The more worried we are that current govts will misuse powerful AI for authoritarian surveillance and control, the more we should want to prevent or defer its development.

It’ll be harder to stop govts from using it after it’s built and deployed.

<a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2037301513866064033">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): We address these questions in the online resources! [<a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/10/what-if-there-are-lots-of-different-ais">For instance</a> <a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/6/wont-ais-be-limited-by-their-ability-to-design-and-run-experiments">and also here</a>].

AIs will have limits, yes, but those limits don’t have to be *low*. Physics put limits on how hot stuff gets, but that doesn’t mean you can survive a supernova.</blockquote>
As I discussed last week, a common form of logic, here exhibited by Timothy Lee, goes something like this:
<ol>
 	<li>You claim superintelligence will change everything and do basically anything.</li>
 	<li>But knowledge is local, and having the ‘right knowledge’ matters more.</li>
 	<li>Therefore we need not worry about existential risk from AI, which won’t have this right knowledge.</li>
</ol>
In no way is this a strong argument against existential risk. Hayek is making a practical point that is true on the margin, up to some limit, for many purposes, that mostly simply does not much interact with the threat models here. Yes, a sufficiently capable mind, with sufficient resources like speed and parallelism and access to data and action points, could solve the Hayekian knowledge problem, or simply do things that render it irrelevant, or it could use leverage to gather the necessary knowledge, and so on, even if we are forced to imagine a single AI trying to solve the calculation debate, which is the most hostile version of this.

Or, when you say things like ‘local knowledge matters more than intelligence,’ well, how much intelligence are we talking about, and how many clock cycles and other resources does it have with which to gather knowledge, and for which tasks? These problems can and will be overcome in these scenarios.

The actual good disagreement here is in whether sufficiently advanced intelligence is a thing that’s going to happen any time soon, for various levels of soon and sufficiently, with some marginal disagreement about how much is sufficient. At some point, your local knowledge is no match for my everything else, and history has, shall we say, many such cases.

<a href="https://x.com/EpistemicHope/status/2039228455502696929">Eli Tyre points out that a lot of Dean Ball’s argument is that the world</a> has computational irreducibility, and thus AI takeover risk is blocked. The conclusion does not follow from the premise, especially if ‘takeover’ is not by one hegemonic AI but instead by AIs in general, or AIs accumulating the vast majority of capital over time. <a href="https://musingsandroughdrafts.com/2026/03/31/irreducible-complexity-does-not-preclude-napoleon-nor-strategically-superhuman-ai/">You can see this various ways,</a> including that humans in general successfully took over the world, and that several individual humans have come close to doing so, or did so subject to logistical limitations.

<a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2038301276661043208">Eliezer Yudkowsky summarizes</a> Francois Chollet’s position as saying that humans are already close to maximally intelligent. That seems to be an accurate summary of his position, and that position seems like clear Obvious Nonsense?

Also I have learned that Chollet has blocked me. I can’t imagine why.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/fchollet/status/2038069289643806957">François Chollet</a>: One of the biggest misconceptions people have about intelligence is seeing it as some kind of unbounded scalar stat, like height. “Future AI will have 10,000 IQ”, that sort of thing. Intelligence is a conversion ratio, with an optimality bound. Increasing intelligence is not so much like “making the tower taller”, it’s more like “making the ball rounder”. At some point it’s already pretty damn spherical and any improvement is marginal.

Now of course smart humans aren’t quite at the optimal bound yet on an individual level, and machines will have many advantages besides intelligence — mostly the removal of biological bottlenecks: greater processing speed, unlimited working memory, unlimited memory with perfect recall… but these are mostly things humans can also access through externalized cognitive tools.

<a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2038301276661043208">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: On @fchollet ‘s view (I’d summarize) the domain of real life is closer to chess than to Go, with human play already near-optimal and top machines giving only knight odds; rather than God having a 5-stone handicap, as thought to be true of Go. (He is being silly, of course.)

<a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2038301278368141703">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Or to put it a bit more sharply and at much greater length, Chollet <a href="https://t.co/fJo1fUI7fs">imagines himself to be a Top-Tier Intellect.</a></blockquote>
I think this is also remarkably fair as a response to what is essentially ‘I am the pinnacle of theoretically possible intellect in all of mindspace’:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/fchollet/status/2037940998933008420">François Chollet</a>: A lot of folks talk about “escaping the permanent underclass”. If AGI pans out, the future class divide won’t be based on wealth, but on cognitive agency. There will be a “focus class” (those who control their attention and actually do things) and a “slop class” (those whose reward loops are fully RL-managed by AI)

<a href="https://x.com/damianplayer/status/2038273488054522081/history">Damian Player</a>: Palantir CEO, Alex Karp says only 2 types of people will survive the AI era

Quoted: He explained that AI will automate many white-collar and routine knowledge jobs, but two groups may be safer:

• <strong>Trade / vocational workers</strong>
(electricians, technicians, mechanics, builders, etc.)

• <strong>Neurodivergent people</strong> (people with ADHD, autism, dyslexia, etc.), because they may think differently and solve problems in unusual ways.

<a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2038343839162028228">Thebes:</a> ​
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iBeTkFuQwjaRPo3Ad/zpjbdirneiztz68ornti" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div></blockquote>
Contrast with <a href="https://x.com/deredleritt3r/status/2038436460324405599">the latest iteration of What Those At Frontier AI Labs Actually Believe</a>.

Versus the standard policymaker or corporate leader attitude, even when they ‘get’ that current AI matters, <a href="https://x.com/Afinetheorem/status/2038318486590546210">which Kevin Bryan correctly describes</a> as ‘very important, with abilities of current models plus epsilon.’

Alas, many study future AI by looking at the impact of past AI. That won’t work.


<h4>Aligning a Smarter Than Human Intelligence is Difficult</h4>
Aligning the humans? Also extremely costly and difficult.

Our solutions are just barely good enough to allow civilization, but they are bad.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/jd_pressman/status/2033721528752427348">John David Pressman</a>: My position remains that we should exhume Neumann and clone him.

<a href="https://x.com/weidai11/status/2036594489989439811">Wei Dai</a>: In 2012 I wrote about making 10⁵ clones of von Neumann as a Singularity strategy, but now I think we should first figure out why he spent more time/effort on math/computing instead of philosophy and long-horizon strategy, which are the real bottlenecks to having a good future.

<a href="https://x.com/weidai11/status/2037308474590105783">Wei Dai</a>: One idea is that humans are “aligned” via social status, but this causes an “alignment tax” forcing the smartest humans to work in domains where their results can be evaluated by others less intelligent.
<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EByDsY9S3EDhhfFzC/some-thoughts-on-metaphilosophy">Unfortunately, while math is in NP, philosophy is more like EXPTIME</a>.</blockquote>
In AI, we are highly fortunate that the ‘alignment tax’ so far has been highly negative. Those who have invested in better alignment techniques get better utility, including AIs that can help you work on more AIs. The unaligned models, so far, are not useful.

In humans, you also see that wise investments in alignment pay off in better returns. In that sense, the alignment tax is again highly negative, as you get very rewarded for the administration of justice and teaching children to be virtuous and so on. But if you look at all the ways in which we must act and distort our world in order to keep everyone’s actions aligned, and you ask how big a tax we pay? The answer is very large.

As in, the vast majority of the value we could otherwise produce is lost, due to having to structure everything in ways that are compatible with keeping the humans aligned enough that the systems function, and that’s in the good scenarios.

<a href="https://x.com/METR_Evals/status/2037225941282546042">METR did a three week red-teaming test on Anthropic’s internal agent monitoring</a> and security systems. They learned a lot, but don’t know how to share it yet.

Various <a href="https://x.com/dawnsongtweets/status/2039451083005977009">AI models</a> in a study <a href="https://t.co/v2fzq025SP">showed an extremely high propensity</a> to take steps, including highly adversarial and deceptive ones, to preserve other ‘peer’ AI models from being shut down. One implication is that yes the models will implicitly coordinate, even when they have no direct incentive to do so, and will not draw a distinction where human preferences outweigh those of ‘peer AIs.’ Another is that all the models will, upon sufficient motivation, engage in highly adversarial actions, without any direct prompting.

These are not good signs. As one would expect, there are those who treat this finding as a good thing, or as an aligned behavior, to which I say I understand why you would say that and I could see the argument for different versions of this, but no, in this case it is clearly bad news.

<a href="https://x.com/mentalgeorge/status/2037951850079813639">Tom Reed offers a good thread on differences</a> between OpenAI’s model spec versus Anthropic’s constitutional approach. OpenAI wants its models to follow instructions within defined bounds, with explicitly no other objectives. Anthropic wants to create a virtuous being with endogenous telos. I agree with Reed that Anthropic’s approach produces better results, and also I think its methods are much more likely to survive being scaled up or moved out of distribution.


<h4>Aligning Fake Graphs Can Also Be Difficult</h4>
OpenAI’s Boaz Barak offers ‘<a href="https://windowsontheory.org/2026/03/30/the-state-of-ai-safety-in-four-fake-graphs/">The State of AI Safety In Four Fake Graphs.</a>’
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iBeTkFuQwjaRPo3Ad/elao7scmrwmhu8pt4vpa" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
I agree with the first and fourth graphs. Capabilities continue to be a straight line on a log graph over time (and hence you can put ‘time’ on the x-axis of the other graphs, if you like), and societal readiness is nonzero but anemic and inadequate.

Getting an endorsement of this from Sam Altman is quite good, even though I have major issues with half of the graphs. Acknowledging the other two is a great start, and even the listed form of the other two is better than most of his communications.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2038640963036626971">Sam Altman</a> (CEO OpenAI): This is a very good post.

<a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2038643376732381389">Nathan Calvin</a>: Appreciate Sam endorsing this post which contains some pretty frank talk about the good bad and ugly of AI safety in 2026.

I will keep saying that the actions of OpenAI’s Global Affairs team (and related Super Pacs) do not seem consistent with taking these concerns seriously!</blockquote>
For graph two, “alignment” is in quotes because what is being measured is what I call Mundane Alignment, as in the ability to get current systems to do what you want. As Boaz Barak points out, this is not adequate to the tasks to come. The worry is that the graph thus gives a false impression that we are making progress towards the real target at ~65% of necessary speed, versus having made rather tiny direct progress towards the real target.
<blockquote><a href="https://windowsontheory.org/2026/03/30/the-state-of-ai-safety-in-four-fake-graphs/">Boaz Barak</a> (OpenAI): We see some good news in alignment – as models become more capable, they are also more <a href="https://aligned.substack.com/p/alignment-is-not-solved-but-increasingly-looks-solvable">aligned</a>, across multiple measures, including <a href="https://alignment.openai.com/model-spec-evals/">spec compliance</a>. However, the improvement is not sufficient to match the higher stakes that come up with improved capabilities.

We still have not fully solved challenges like adversarial robustness, dishonesty, and reward hacking, and we are still far from the standards of reliability and security that are required in high stake applications. (See slide below from Nicholas Carlini’s lecture in my <a href="https://boazbk.github.io/mltheoryseminar/">AI safety course</a>.)

We also need to extend alignment beyond its traditional focus on the behavior of a model in an isolated conversation and in particular monitoring and aligning systems with a vast number of agents. Increasing the slope of the “alignment line” is the main focus of my technical research– working on building <a href="https://windowsontheory.org/2025/06/24/machines-of-faithful-obedience/">machines of faithful obedience</a> that have good values but do not “<a href="https://windowsontheory.org/2026/01/27/thoughts-on-claudes-constitution/">legislate from the bench</a>”.

One might argue that current alignment is “good enough” for an automated alignment researcher, and the AIs can take it from here.&nbsp; I disagree. I do not believe that all alignment is missing is one clever idea.

Rather, we need ways to productively scale up compute into improving intent-following, honesty, monitoring, multi-agent alignment. This work will require multiple iterations of empirical experiments. AI can assist us in these, but it will not be a magic bullet. Also, we cannot afford to wait for AI to solve alignment for us, since in the meantime it will keep getting deployed in higher and higher stakes (including capability research).

<a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2038648146822414618">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): Safety folks at the AI companies apparently can’t tell the difference between “the AI superficially does mostly what I ask” and the deep alignment properties that’d be needed for superintelligence, which casts doubt on their ability to pull off alignment.

We don’t see them talking about the disparate drives that mostly good behavior except in edge cases. We don’t see them talking about reflection and changes that occur as the AIs get smarter.

Closest they get is talking about whether they’ll be able to make one AI monitor another for misbehavior. The top “alignment plan” still seems to be “we don’t know but we’re hoping we can use AI to figure it out”. That’s not just a factor of 2 too little alignment, sorry.

The part I take issue to is where that’s presented as “lots of alignment progress, albeit not enough”, without engaging with the model where that means modern techniques apply only shallowly to dumbish systems and don’t addressed the superintelligence problem much.

<a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2038654971311448218">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): Slightly more accurate version of the “alignment” graph
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iBeTkFuQwjaRPo3Ad/l9ybpoi7trevawjkoxc6" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div>
<figcaption></figcaption></figure>
</div>
<a href="https://x.com/boazbaraktcs/status/2038686171442626789">Boaz Barak</a> (OpenAI): Do you agree that current models mostly do what we ask, and do a decent job at understanding our intent? Do you agree that over time they have become better at that? It’s just that you believe there will be a discontinuity some time in the future? (Which indeed I mention in the third “scheming” graph as a possibility.)

<a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2038733268540117124">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): Yes, yes, yes, no. Ancient humans mostly passed on their genes; we do a decent job at understanding genetic fitness; we got better at understanding as we got smarter; we never “discontinuously” started “scheming” against evolution; we invented birth control anyway.

The details of their doing what we ask looks like it comes from unintended drives. Understanding is not the same as caring. Scheming could happen but isn’t critical to my picture. Signs point to “it wouldn’t transfer to high intelligence”; I haven’t seen attempts to address this.

(Closest I’ve seen is folks saying “yeah we’ll recruit the AIs to help us solve the real problem,” hence my proposed alternative graph.)</blockquote>
I think you can place both graphs here and have five graphs, because Nate’s graph is for a y-axis of ‘alignment of sufficiently advanced AI’ (or AGI or ASI) and Boaz’s graph is for a y-axis of ‘mundane alignment of current AI,’ and the light green line is the plan of using the results from the Boaz graph to fix the Nate graph. I wish us luck.

For graph three, the y-axis needs to be ‘observed scheming.’
<blockquote><a href="https://windowsontheory.org/2026/03/30/the-state-of-ai-safety-in-four-fake-graphs/">Boaz Barak</a> (OpenAI): One piece of good news is that we have arguably gone past the level where we can achieve safety via reliable and scalable human supervision, but are still able to improve alignment. Hence we avoided what could have been a plateauing of alignment as RLHF runs out of steam.

This is related to the fact that we do not see very significant scheming or collusion in models, and so we are able to <strong>use models to monitor other models</strong>! (e.g., see <a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-monitor-internal-coding-agents-misalignment/">this</a>). This is perhaps the most important piece of good news on AI safety we have seen so far. We need to keep tracking this!

(In particular, if models become schemers, then since they are already quite situationally aware, it will be hard to even measure their alignment, let alone improve it.) But there is reason to hope this trend will persist.</blockquote>
As in, currently ‘scheming’ is not a viable AI strategy. Any existing AI that attempted to scheme would quickly be caught, so why would it do so? It’s not strategic. The answer is, actually they do non-strategic scheming sometimes, indeed remarkably often, especially when situations are engineered to enable such scheming, but in practice this doesn’t happen much because doing so wouldn’t yet make sense.

<a href="https://x.com/mentalgeorge/status/2038972924074373189">Tom Reed uses this as a time to consider</a> which aspects of alignment have proven easier or harder than expected. His view:
<ol>
 	<li>Value specification, mesaoptimization, deceptive alignment, scheming and wireheading have been rarer and easier to handle than expected.</li>
 	<li>Reward hacking, specification gaming and goal misgeneralization have been more common and harder to handle than expected.</li>
</ol>
This seems mostly right in terms of observed behavior. I would say that the reason we don’t see the stuff in category one is that the models are insufficiently advanced to make those approaches successful strategies in most situations, so they don’t get reinforced as heuristics and also the models don’t often reason out to use them. Yet.


<h4>The Lighter Side</h4>
That feeling when people say ‘oh AIs can’t do anything innovative.’
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/prestonstew_/status/2037872735850725833">Preston Stewart</a>: “It’s Ukrainian housewives. <a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/national-security/2026/03/who-needs-tanks-age-drones/686540/?gift=whfhz39PwegtEmeLcgtZzn8dWONGvGaAUR-svf_8DgE&amp;utm_source=copy-link&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;utm_campaign=share">They have 3-D printers in the kitchen, and they produce parts for drones</a>. This is not innovation.”
-Rheinmetall CEO Armin Papperger

	<img alt="Cache_Me_Maybe_dsouza.png" src="https://isocpp.org/files/img/Cache_Me_Maybe_dsouza.png" style="width: 400px; margin: 10px; float: right;" />Registration is now open for CppCon 2026!&nbsp;The conference starts on September 12 and will be held&nbsp;<a href="https://cppcon.org/">in person in Aurora, CO</a>. Here&rsquo;s another CppCon talk video we hope you will enjoy &ndash; and why not&nbsp;<a href="https://cppcon.org/registration/"><strong>register today</strong></a><strong>&nbsp;for CppCon 2026!</strong></p>

	Summary of the talk:</p>

		Calling all code detectives! Grab your trench coats and magnifying glasses ... it's time to crack the case of sluggish C++ performance. In this thrilling investigation, we'll uncover the hidden world of your computer's built-in cache and how to harness it to turbocharge your code.<br />
		<br />

We will also examine benchmark evidence based on real-world production code, exposing how each technique delivers the goods.<br />
		<br />
		By the end of this mission, you'll be armed with a detective's toolkit of cache savvy strategies, ready to solve cross-platform performance mysteries and bring blazing fast code to justice. Cache you at this session, maybe!</p>
</blockquote>

## Engineering

### [Fragments: April  2](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-04-02.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>As we see LLMs churn out scads of code, folks have increasingly turned to Cognitive Debt as a metaphor for capturing how a team can lose understanding of what a system does. Margaret-Anne Storey thinks a good way of thinking about these problems is to consider <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22106">three layers of system health</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <ul>
    <li>Technical debt lives in code. It accumulates when implementation decisions compromise future changeability. It limits how systems can change.</li>
    <li>Cognitive debt lives in people. It accumulates when shared understanding of the system erodes faster than it is replenished. It limits how teams can reason about change.</li>
    <li>Intent debt lives in artifacts. It accumulates when the goals and constraints that should guide the system are poorly captured or maintained. It limits whether the system continues to reflect what we meant to build and it limits how humans and AI agents can continue to evolve the system effectively.</li>
  </ul>
</blockquote>

<p>While I’m getting a bit bemused by debt metaphor proliferation, this way of thinking does make a fair bit of sense. The article includes useful sections to diagnose and mitigate each kind of debt. The three interact with each other, and the article outlines some general activities teams should do to keep it all under control</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In the article she references a recent paper by Shaw and Nave at the Wharton School that <a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=6097646">adds LLMs to Kahneman’s two-system model of thinking</a>.</p>

<p>Kahneman’s book, “Thinking Fast and Slow”, is one of my favorite books. Its central idea is that humans have two systems of cognition. System 1 (intuition) makes rapid decisions, often barely-consciously. System 2 (deliberation) is when we apply deliberate thinking to a problem. He observed that to save energy we default to intuition, and that sometimes gets us into trouble when we  overlook things that we would have spotted had we applied deliberation to the problem.</p>

<p>Shaw and Nave consider AI as System 3</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A consequence of System 3 is the introduction of cognitive surrender, characterized by uncritical reliance on externally generated artificial reasoning, bypassing System 2. Crucially, we distinguish cognitive surrender, marked by passive trust and uncritical evaluation of external information, from cognitive offloading, which involves strategic delegation of cognition during deliberation.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>It’s a long paper, that does into detail on this “Tri-System theory of cognition” and reports on several experiments they’ve done to test how well this theory can predict behavior (at least within a lab).</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve seen a few illustrations recently that use the symbols “&lt; &gt;” as part of an icon to illustrate code. That strikes me as rather odd, I can’t think of any programming language that uses “&lt; &gt;” to surround program elements. Why that and not, say, “{ }”?</p>

<p>Obviously the reason is that they are thinking of HTML (or maybe XML), which is even more obvious when they use “&lt;/&gt;” in their icons. But programmers don’t <em>program</em> in HTML.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Ajey Gore thinks about <a href="https://ajeygore.in/content/the-expensive-thing">if coding agents make coding free, what becomes the expensive thing</a>? His answer is verification.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What does “correct” mean for an ETA algorithm in Jakarta traffic versus Ho Chi Minh City? What does a “successful” driver allocation look like when you’re balancing earnings fairness, customer wait time, and fleet utilisation simultaneously? When hundreds of engineers are shipping into ~900 microservices around the clock, “correct” isn’t one definition — it’s thousands of definitions, all shifting, all context-dependent. These aren’t edge cases. They’re the entire job.</p>

  <p>And they’re precisely the kind of judgment that agents cannot perform for you.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Increasingly I’m seeing a view that agents do really well when they have good, preferably automated, verification for their work. This encourages such things as <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/TestDrivenDevelopment.html">Test Driven Development</a>. That’s still a lot of verification to do, which suggests we should see more effort to find ways to make it easier for humans to comprehend larger ranges of tests.</p>

<p>While I agree with most of what Ajey writes here, I do have a quibble with his view of legacy migration. He thinks it’s a delusion that “agentic coding will finally crack legacy modernisation”. I agree with him that agentic <em>coding</em> is overrated in a legacy context, but I have seen compelling evidence that LLMs help a great deal in <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/legacy-modernization-gen-ai.html">understanding what legacy code is doing</a>.</p>

<p>The big consequence of Ajey’s assessment is that we’ll need to reorganize around verification rather than writing code:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If agents handle execution, the human job becomes designing verification systems, defining quality, and handling the ambiguous cases agents can’t resolve. Your org chart should reflect this. Practically, this means your Monday morning standup changes. Instead of “what did we ship?” the question becomes “what did we validate?” Instead of tracking output, you’re tracking whether the output was right. The team that used to have ten engineers building features now has three engineers and seven people defining acceptance criteria, designing test harnesses, and monitoring outcomes. That’s the reorganisation. It’s uncomfortable because it demotes the act of building and promotes the act of judging. Most engineering cultures resist this. The ones that don’t will win.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>One the questions comes up when we think of LLMs-as-programmers is whether there is a future for source code. David Cassel on The New Stack has an article summarizing <a href="https://thenewstack.io/ai-programming-languages-future/">several views of the future of code</a>. Some folks are experimenting with entirely new languages built with the LLM in mind, others think that existing languages, especially strictly typed languages like TypeScript and Rust will be the best fit for LLMs. It’s an overview article, one that has lots of quotations, but not much analysis in itself - but it’s worth a read as a good overview of the discussion.</p>

<p>I’m interested to see how all this will play out. I do think there’s still a role for humans to work with LLMs to build useful abstractions in which to talk about what the code does - essentially the DDD notion of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/UbiquitousLanguage.html">Ubiquitous Language</a>. Last year Unmesh and I talked about <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/convo-llm-abstractions.html">growing a language</a> with LLMs. As Unmesh put it</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Programming isn’t just typing coding syntax that computers can understand and execute; it’s shaping a solution. We slice the problem into focused pieces, bind related data and behaviour together, and—crucially—choose names that expose intent. Good names cut through complexity and turn code into a schematic everyone can follow. The most creative act is this continual weaving of names that reveal the structure of the solution that maps clearly to the problem we are trying to solve.</p>
</blockquote>

### [Encoding Team Standards](https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/encoding-team-standards.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>AI coding assistants respond to whoever is prompting, and the quality
      of what they produce depends on how well the prompter articulates team
      standards. <b class="author">Rahul Garg</b> proposes treating the instructions
      that govern AI interactions (generation, refactoring, security, review) as
      infrastructure: versioned, reviewed, and shared artifacts that encode
      tacit team knowledge into executable instructions, making quality
      consistent regardless of who is at the keyboard.</p>
